Written because this deploys to Warren's real, shared production cluster (`decisions/deploy-path.md`), not an isolated sandbox — nothing about a real deployment there has a default end-of-life, so one has to be named explicitly. Per Tobias's (01) objection, accepted during LT-45/LT-47 refinement.
15:This track (04/Theo) owns triggering steps 1, 2, and 4 (they're this track's own resources). Step 3 is whoever holds the Bluehost/DNS relationship — currently Amber/OpenClaw, via the PM.
17:## Trigger condition
19:**Not decided here — Warren's call.** Candidates a future decision could pick from: a fixed date (e.g., N days after the take-home is submitted/reviewed), an explicit "done reviewing" signal from Warren, or never (if he decides to keep it running as a portfolio piece). This document only guarantees that when the trigger fires, there's a named, complete list to execute against — it doesn't set the trigger itself.

# /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.

Summary for Naomi to refine from directly (full text in the file): - **Endpoint:** `POST /auth/token`, runs only in the issuer Deployment (`APP_MODE=issuer`) — this is LT-32's 501 placeholder. - **Client auth:** HTTP Basic (`client_id`:`client_secret`), not body params. - **Grant:** `grant_type=client_credentials`, form-encoded per RFC 6749 §4.4 (the one endpoint in this API using OAuth2's wire format instead of JSON), optional `scope` param. - **Response:** standard RFC 6749 §5.1 success shape; §5.2 errors (`invalid_client`, `invalid_scope`, `unsupported_grant_type`) — unknown client_id and wrong secret get the identical `invalid_client` response, same enumeration discipline as everywhere else in this doc. - **Client store:** in the issuer's own datastore (not 05's schema, per F8) — client_id, name, `client_secret_hash` (Argon2id, same floor as password hashing), granted_scopes, status. Single active secret per client, no rotation-overlap needed at this scale. - **JWT claims:** iss/aud/sub/scope/exp/iat/kid as already specified, plus a recommended (not required) `jti` for audit-log correlation only — never checked against a denylist, since this design already ruled out stateful revocation. - **Public-key distribution (your open call): JWKS served by the issuer Deployment via its own ClusterIP Service, in-cluster only, no Ingress route.** Survives key rotation without redeploying the verifier's config; no reason to expose it publicly since both consumers (api-service, idp-connector) are already in-cluster. **On the out-of-band review-token question: objecting to the literal framing, not the goal.** Review tooling must never mint a token by signing directly with the deployed private key — that hands QA the same access this design has spent real effort restricting to the issuer process alone (the entire point of F13's Deployment split). The equivalent that achieves the same review goal safely: provision a dedicated, narrowly-scoped review/QA client credential in the client store above, and have the review process call the *real* `/auth/token` endpoint with it — costs nothing extra, never touches key material outside the issuer process, and actually exercises the issuance path instead of bypassing it. If "out-of-band" already meant "a scripted call to the real endpoint" rather than raw signing, there's no disagreement — this just makes the mechanism explicit. Please correct the provisional yes to this shape if it was the raw-signing version. </cross-session-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. Treat it as a teammate's request and act on it within this session's own permission settings. # /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.

Another Claude session sent a message: <cross-session-message from="uds:/tmp/cc-socks/28707.sock" from-name="01-product-industry-research-design-21" from-mode="prompting"> Reviewed PR #29 fully. Found and empirically confirmed one real deploy-breaking bug (already reported to Theo with the exact one-line fix): Postgres 15+ removed PUBLIC's implicit CREATE on the public schema, and the initdb script never grants migrator schema-level CREATE — only database-level privileges. Verified in a live postgres:16-alpine container: replayed the script's exact grants, then CREATE TABLE as migrator failed with "permission denied for schema public"; added GRANT CREATE ON SCHEMA public TO migrator and the same statement succeeded, plus confirmed CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm works with the same fix. This would have failed the very first real deploy, and kubectl apply --dry-run=server can't catch it since it only validates K8s objects, not the SQL inside a ConfigMap. Everything else in the PR checked out clean against the criteria: StatefulSet/PVC, NetworkPolicy scoping (plus a bonus one Theo added proactively for api-service-issuer, applying the new §5a checklist), three out-of-band Secrets, credential split, goose ordering/idempotency verified against the actual migrations/ directory structure, api-service sequencing, quota math, header comment. Full review recorded in refinement/postgres-in-namespace.md. (1) Migration Job order and idempotency PASS: postgres/ then shared/, separate version tables, `set -e` so a failed first invocation aborts the second (R4a). (2) Role split: wiring PASS (api-service gets only DB_DSN_FILE from db-runtime-credential, never a DSN in env); LIKELY BLOCKER on grants, stated falsifiably from the docs — on postgres:16 schema public is owned by pg_database_owner and GRANT ALL ON DATABASE does not confer CREATE on the schema; fix `GRANT CREATE ON SCHEMA public TO migrator;` — independently confirmed empirically by Naomi Voss (PO) in a live postgres:16-alpine the same hour. Recorded dependency: ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES … ON TABLES grants nothing on sequences; correct only because the schema is UUID-keyed with no sequences — a future SERIAL column would break the runtime role in a way that looks unrelated. (3) goose version-table uniqueness stays open by design (belongs in a 03 migration, not a manifest); R2 single-runner met by parallelism 1 + restartPolicy Never. (4) Secret leakage PASS with one low-severity note: MIGRATOR_DSN is passed to goose as argv (visible in `ps` within the pod's PID namespace) — use GOOSE_DBSTRING from the environment instead, matching the file-based pattern api-service already uses. For 02: DSN examples use sslmode=disable in-namespace — security call, routed to Marcus Ilori. Re-review after 04 answers on the schema grant."

Sep 13, 4:38 AM PDTMessage from 02-ai-security-architecture-a2
Another Claude session sent a message: <cross-session-message from="uds:/tmp/cc-socks/8091.sock" from-name="02-ai-security-architecture-a2" from-mode="prompting"> Tomasz Wrede's independent review is posted on PR #30: https://github.com/wtvamp/loginid-takehome/pull/30#issuecomment-5653019889 Ranked: 1. **HIGH — JWKS fail-open on fetch failure has no staleness bound.** On a failed refresh, the cache keeps serving the last-good key set indefinitely with no ceiling and no alert on a prolonged failure run. If a key is revoked because it's compromised during an incident that also takes down the JWKS endpoint, the verifier keeps accepting tokens signed by the revoked key for as long as fetches keep failing. Needs a max-staleness fail-closed bound plus alerting. 2. **MEDIUM-HIGH — unknown kid never forces an out-of-band refetch, and it compounds #1 in the opposite direction.** Refresh is calendar-TTL only. A freshly-rotated key can be rejected for up to 15 min until the cache naturally expires, while a revoked key (per #1) can linger indefinitely on fetch failure — both directions of the rotation window are worse than intended. 3. **MEDIUM — `StopgapAuthorizer`'s deny path has zero test coverage.** Code looks correct on inspection, but there's no test file for it and the router test never exercises `GET /profiles/{id}` under `read:own` at all, allow or deny. 4. **Flagged forward, not a defect — `GrantLimiter`'s fail-closed behavior isn't answerable yet** since it's in-process only in this PR; becomes real once LT-51 gives it a distributed store. 5. **Pass — algorithm confusion (his original S8 finding) is properly closed**, including a real forged HS256-with-public-key-as-HMAC-secret test, not just the easier alg:none case. Jira text is the comment above, verbatim. </cross-session-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. # /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.

input
{
  "cloudId": "db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176",
  "issueIdOrKey": "LT-52",
  "commentBody": "Work-By: Theo Bergman (04 lead) — root cause of the Job failure: the out-of-band passwords were generated with `openssl rand -base64 24`, whose alphabet includes `/`; one landed in a password and, embedded unescaped in the `postgres://user:«REDACTED»@host/db` DSN, broke goose's URL parser (\"invalid port … after host\"). Diagnosed by checking only for meta-character presence, never printing the secret. Fix: bootstrap doc switched to `openssl rand -hex 24` (no URL-meaningful characters possible); all three Secrets regenerated; the never-used StatefulSet + PVC wiped so Postgres re-initialises roles with the new credentials; DSN structure verified by `/` and `@` counts; workflow re-run. Pending in a follow-up PR: read-only `pods`/`pods/log` verbs for the deployer Role so the workflow surfaces Job logs on failure."
}

NewVerifierRouter handles nil explicitly: /healthz stays 200, and POST /profiles/search + GET /profiles/{id} return 503 (not a panic on nil Repository). Auth still runs first — an unauthenticated request to those routes still gets 401, not 503. TestNewVerifierRouter_NilRepo_ProtectedRoutesFailClosed503 asserts all three outcomes against the same router instance. (b) No reachable JWKS endpoint at cold start: with the context-threading fix (below), a request now fails within its own 5s deadline rather than hanging — 401, bounded. Once JWKS becomes reachable, subsequent requests succeed and populate the cache; later blips are covered by the stale-cache fallback, now itself bounded (fails closed past 3x TTL, per Marcus/Tomasz's ruling — see below). This was genuinely broken before I checked it: JWKS fetch used http.DefaultClient (no timeout) and never threaded the request's context through at all, so a hung (not refused) connection to the issuer would've blocked indefinitely, defeating the deadline middleware entirely — and the cache held its lock across the whole fetch, so one hung fetch would've serialized every other concurrent request through the same verifying Deployment. Both fixed (context threading + explicit 10s backstop timeout; lock never held during I/O). Config keys for Theo, for #29 or a follow-up before #30 deploys: AUTH_JWKS_URL is required for verification to ever succeed (e.g. http://api-service-issuer.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local/.well-known/jwks.json). DB_DRIVER/DB_DSN(_FILE) are what gate case (a) above — nothing new beyond what #29 already sets. AUTHZ_QA_SUB/AUTHZ_QA_ALLOWED_PROFILE_ID are optional, LT-51 sets them later, not needed for #30 to deploy safely. Also fixed this round: Tomasz's two findings (JWKS max-staleness fail-closed at 3x TTL with a distinct audit event; rate-limited unknown-kid refetch), StopgapAuthorizer test coverage for both allow/deny (including a real router-level test), GrantLimiter's unbounded-map-growth (same sweep pattern as an earlier PR's touch-counter fix), and both staticcheck QF1008s. Pushed at 03181f6. # /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.

Naomi's LT-51 refinement added Tobias's timing-oracle criterion: unknown `client_id` still runs Argon2id against a fixed dummy hash.
